    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm here because I've been having a really hard time lately with listening to people when they're talking directly to me. It feels like my mind just drifts off, even when I'm trying my best to pay attention to what they are saying.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble paying attention like this since childhood, or did it start more recently? If it started recently, was there a particular stressor or did it come along with feeling depressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I've actually struggled with this since I was a kid, definitely before I turned 12. It didn't start because of recent stress or anxiety, though it has become much more of a noticeable problem for me over the last year and eight months.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems cause difficulties in at least two areas of your life, like at work or school and at home or in relationships? Can you give an example?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about it in terms of specific areas like work or home. To be honest, I'm not entirely sure and haven't paid close attention to how it impacts those different domains specifically.\n\nDoctor: Besides drifting off when people are talking, do you often make careless mistakes, have trouble organizing tasks, lose things, forget appointments, or avoid tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: I definitely lose important things all the time, like my keys, wallet, or phone. However, I don't really experience the other things you mentioned, like making careless mistakes, struggling with organization, or avoiding tasks that require a lot of mental effort.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often forget daily tasks or appointments, get easily distracted by things around you, or have trouble following through on instructions even when you understand them?\n\nPatient: I get easily distracted by almost anything around me, whether it's a background noise, someone walking past, or even just my own random thoughts. However, I don't really struggle with forgetting appointments or following through on instructions.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me whether these attention and distraction problems have clearly caused trouble in two different settings, such as at work or school and also at home or in relationships?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure, to be honest. I haven't really thought about whether these issues clearly cause trouble in two different settings like work and home.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school as a child, did these attention problems lead to issues like teachers saying you weren’t listening, getting distracted in class, or forgetting homework?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. My teachers back then would constantly complain that I wasn't listening to them when they spoke to me directly. I was also incredibly easily distracted by anything happening in the classroom, like someone tapping a pencil or looking out the window.\n\nDoctor: Can you give one specific recent example of how your attention problems caused trouble at work or school, and one example at home or in a relationship?\n\nPatient: At work, I constantly get sidetracked by background noises or my own unrelated thoughts, which makes it really hard to finish my tasks on time. At home, my partner gets very frustrated with me because I often don't seem to be listening at all when they are speaking directly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also tend to feel restless, fidget a lot, talk excessively, interrupt others, or act impulsively, or is the problem mainly inattention?"

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
